```text
66	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}
67	\label{sec:cross_k}
68	
69	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ significance pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 1/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with mixed effects (3/12) and wild bootstrap (4/12) falling in between; fractional logit with clustered SE gives $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 9/12. The $K{=}3$ ``co-determination'' pattern thus depends on the inference method chosen, and we characterize it as a method-sensitive pattern rather than a robust finding. This contrasts sharply with $K{\geq}5$, where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all inference methods (bootstrap 10/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).
70	
71	A natural question is whether this pattern persists at larger panel sizes. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
72	
73	\begin{figure}[t]
74	\centering
75	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
76	\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 0/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 10/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
77	\label{fig:regime_transition}
78	\end{figure}
79	
80	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 10/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 6/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 10/12 at $K{=}5$ to 9/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
81

16	\paragraph{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}
17	$\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\rho{=}0.978$, $|\bar{\beta}|{=}0.497$), it outperforms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\ 9--10/12, $|\bar{\beta}|{=}0.430$; Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), whereas $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \to 10 \to 9$), suggesting MI-based effective independence captures redundancy-driven vulnerability distinct from pairwise agreement. The SDPI bound ($\eta_{\mathrm{joint}} \leq K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\kappa$-diversity lacks. The shift may partly reflect improved MI estimation at larger $K$; distinguishing estimation artifacts from structural effects requires larger judge pools. Full comparison with Q-statistic, disagreement, and $\kappa$-diversity across all $K$ values is in Appendix~\ref{app:alt_diversity} (Table~\ref{tab:alt_diversity}).
18

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0116N7aEVTCGLg19buKr4X6r`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}
4	LLM judges gained traction after \citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \citet{dawid1979maximum}; \citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\% to 10--19\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent LLM systems but focus on agent coordination rather than judge panel composition.
5	
6	\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}
7	\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. CJT explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected, since the adversary optimizes against the aggregate decision boundary rather than individual voters---a point formalized in ensemble adversarial training \citep{tramer2018ensemble} and gradient-diversity defenses \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. Our contribution is not this well-known direction (diversity widens the joint attack surface across heterogeneous models), but the \emph{panel-size-dependent structure}: the predictor shift from both predictors contributing at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\geq}5$.
8	
9	\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}
10	\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (10/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.
11	
12	\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}
13	Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. The key differences between vision ensembles and LLM judge panels are: (1)~adversaries manipulate natural-language inputs rather than gradient-based perturbations, (2)~panel decisions are discrete votes rather than continuous feature combinations, and (3)~aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter rather than a fixed architecture. These differences motivate our empirical approach: theoretical results from vision ensemble robustness do not directly transfer to the LLM judge setting.
14	
15	\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}
16	Classical BFT \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI) to measure judge correlation, weighting agreement patterns by item difficulty so that correlated failures on hard items are penalized more heavily. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension: Gaussian copula MI quantifies pairwise statistical independence regardless of item difficulty, connecting directly to the SDPI vulnerability bound. Their de-entangled reweighting and our Bayesian calibration baseline both mitigate judge correlation, but through different mechanisms: reweighting decomposes correlation along a difficulty axis and downweights entangled judges, whereas Bayesian calibration shrinks each judge's influence toward a clean-accuracy prior. Credibility scoring \citep{ebrahimi2025adversary} offers a third complementary strategy by penalizing judges whose scores deviate from consensus.
17	
18	\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}
19	Information-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.
20	
```

Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attack types ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}",
          "-$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\\rho{=}0.978$, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.497$), it outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 9--10/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.430$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), whereas $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \\to 10 \\to 9$), suggesting MI-based effective independence captures redundancy-driven vulnerability distinct from pairwise agreement. The SDPI bound ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks. The shift may partly reflect improved MI estimation at larger $K$; distinguishing estimation artifacts from structural effects requires larger judge pools. Full comparison with Q-statistic, disagreement, and $\\kappa$-diversity across all $K$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:alt_diversity} (Table~\\ref{tab:alt_diversity}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent LLM systems but focus on agent coordination rather than judge panel composition.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. CJT explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected, since the adversary optimizes against the aggregate decision boundary rather than individual voters---a point formalized in ensemble adversarial training \\citep{tramer2018ensemble} and gradient-diversity defenses \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. Our contribution is not this well-known direction (diversity widens the joint attack surface across heterogeneous models), but the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure}: the predictor shift from both predictors contributing at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (10/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. The key differences between vision ensembles and LLM judge panels are: (1)~adversaries manipulate natural-language inputs rather than gradient-based perturbations, (2)~panel decisions are discrete votes rather than continuous feature combinations, and (3)~aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter rather than a fixed architecture. These differences motivate our empirical approach: theoretical results from vision ensemble robustness do not directly transfer to the LLM judge setting.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI) to measure judge correlation, weighting agreement patterns by item difficulty so that correlated failures on hard items are penalized more heavily. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension: Gaussian copula MI quantifies pairwise statistical independence regardless of item difficulty, connecting directly to the SDPI vulnerability bound. Their de-entangled reweighting and our Bayesian calibration baseline both mitigate judge correlation, but through different mechanisms: reweighting decomposes correlation along a difficulty axis and downweights entangled judges, whereas Bayesian calibration shrinks each judge's influence toward a clean-accuracy prior. Credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary} offers a third complementary strategy by penalizing judges whose scores deviate from consensus.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

Our contribution is not this well-known direction (diversity widens the joint attack surface across heterogeneous models), but the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure}: the predictor shift from both predictors contributing at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.",
    "newString": "CJT explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \\citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. Our contribution is not this well-known direction but the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure}: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent LLM systems but focus on agent coordination rather than judge panel composition.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. CJT explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected, since the adversary optimizes against the aggregate decision boundary rather than individual voters---a point formalized in ensemble adversarial training \\citep{tramer2018ensemble} and gradient-diversity defenses \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. Our contribution is not this well-known direction (diversity widens the joint attack surface across heterogeneous models), but the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure}: the predictor shift from both predictors contributing at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (10/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. The key differences between vision ensembles and LLM judge panels are: (1)~adversaries manipulate natural-language inputs rather than gradient-based perturbations, (2)~panel decisions are discrete votes rather than continuous feature combinations, and (3)~aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter rather than a fixed architecture. These differences motivate our empirical approach: theoretical results from vision ensemble robustness do not directly transfer to the LLM judge setting.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via Gaussian copula MI, connecting directly to the SDPI bound. Three mitigation strategies address judge correlation through different mechanisms: DBEI-based reweighting, our Bayesian calibration baseline, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

  "old_string": "Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ significance pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 1/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with mixed effects (3/12) and wild bootstrap (4/12) falling in between; fractional logit with clustered SE gives $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 9/12. The $K{=}3$ ``co-determination'' pattern thus depends on the inference method chosen, and we characterize it as a method-sensitive pattern rather than a robust finding. This contrasts sharply with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all inference methods (bootstrap 10/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).\n\nA natural question is whether this pattern persists at larger panel sizes. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.",
  "new_string": "Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 1/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 10/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction."
}

Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels. The distribution of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes is reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist} (Table~\\ref{tab:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. Point estimates remain uniformly positive for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, but clustered SE confidence intervals are wide at this panel size ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12 significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 8/12  & 1/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\ % TODO: V1 values — recompute with V2 data when server available\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 1/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), demonstrating that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive. All five methods preserve the uniformly positive coefficient direction for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction is robust across all methods. Random subsampling further confirms stability (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ significance pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 1/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with mixed effects (3/12) and wild bootstrap (4/12) falling in between; fractional logit with clustered SE gives $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 9/12. The $K{=}3$ ``co-determination'' pattern thus depends on the inference method chosen, and we characterize it as a method-sensitive pattern rather than a robust finding. This contrasts sharply with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all inference methods (bootstrap 10/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).\n\nA natural question is whether this pattern persists at larger panel sizes. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 0/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 10/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 10/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 6/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 10/12 at $K{=}5$ to 9/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (10/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Uniform Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\\% at $K{=}5$; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved under majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense.\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LQRi44r72sNEA4enPDii52`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We document a predictor shift in LLM judge panel vulnerability: at $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ alone is significant (10/12), uniformly positive. Fractional logit regression confirms the positive association across most conditions ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12). Aggregation influences predictability ($R^2$: 0.83--0.09). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}) fundamentally limits causal separation, with Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values failing to exceed the confound in 78\% of conditions: screen $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}3$, manage independence at $K{\geq}5$; causal identification requires diversity control \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}.
4	
```

    "oldString": "The key differences between vision ensembles and LLM judge panels are: (1)~adversaries manipulate natural-language inputs rather than gradient-based perturbations, (2)~panel decisions are discrete votes rather than continuous feature combinations, and (3)~aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter rather than a fixed architecture. These differences motivate our empirical approach: theoretical results from vision ensemble robustness do not directly transfer to the LLM judge setting.",
    "newString": "Three differences motivate our empirical approach: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, panel decisions are discrete votes, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent LLM systems but focus on agent coordination rather than judge panel composition.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. CJT explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \\citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. Our contribution is not this well-known direction but the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure}: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (10/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. The key differences between vision ensembles and LLM judge panels are: (1)~adversaries manipulate natural-language inputs rather than gradient-based perturbations, (2)~panel decisions are discrete votes rather than continuous feature combinations, and (3)~aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter rather than a fixed architecture. These differences motivate our empirical approach: theoretical results from vision ensemble robustness do not directly transfer to the LLM judge setting.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via Gaussian copula MI, connecting directly to the SDPI bound. Three mitigation strategies address judge correlation through different mechanisms: DBEI-based reweighting, our Bayesian calibration baseline, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

Actually, let me first check if there's any easy line savings elsewhere. The predictor shift passage explains how at K=3 a single compromised judge flips the majority, but at K≥5 the adversary needs to compromise at least 3 judges, and η_max's contribution collapses because no single judge is pivotal anymore. The uniform positive direction passage discusses how K_eff's coefficient is positive across all 12 conditions under OLS and GAM bootstrap, with varying significance depending on the method. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017qitM9s6xM8zdvV25oEdXv`</summary>

```text
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} We provide the first systematic empirical characterization of how panel diversity is associated with adversarial vulnerability in LLM judge panels. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions, confirmed by both OLS and GAM bootstrap (88.9--100\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\% at $K{=}5$). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 78\% of conditions (28/36), though fractional logit with clustered standard errors confirms the positive association ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12). Statistical significance is inference-method-dependent (OLS 12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$). No attack-type-dependent directional split exists (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$; \S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
21	\item \textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the sole significant predictor (10/12 at $K{=}5$, 9/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
22	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules influence how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83 $\to$ Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), providing partial rather than complete defense (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
25	Our findings describe descriptive associations; causal interpretation is fundamentally limited by the diversity--capability collinearity ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$, VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent predictor shift. The positive direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across all 12 conditions (FDR-significant under OLS; GAM bootstrap confirms 88.9--100\\% positive rate) is directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}, but the $K$-dependent transition in predictor dominance is previously uncharacterized. At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ show some significance; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the sole significant predictor while $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to zero significance. This transition arises in the LLM judge setting, where adversaries manipulate through natural language rather than gradient-based perturbations, and aggregation rules provide a practical modulation lever. GAM bootstrap confirms no significant difference between attack types (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$).\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this shift (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Aggregation rules influence predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} We provide the first systematic empirical characterization of how panel diversity is associated with adversarial vulnerability in LLM judge panels. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions, confirmed by both OLS and GAM bootstrap (88.9--100\\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\\% at $K{=}5$). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 78\\% of conditions (28/36), though fractional logit with clustered standard errors confirms the positive association ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12). Statistical significance is inference-method-dependent (OLS 12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$). No attack-type-dependent directional split exists (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$; \\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the sole significant predictor (10/12 at $K{=}5$, 9/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules influence how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83 $\\to$ Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), providing partial rather than complete defense (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings describe descriptive associations; causal interpretation is fundamentally limited by the diversity--capability collinearity ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$, VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n",

3. 解释 K=3 attenuation：df=14 + t-distribution 对只有 455 panels 的 K=3 极端保守，raw p-values 仍有 5/12 显著

_source line 672 · 2026-05-24T12:27:56.900Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01P9ySTgJozeZSYBdQLu3NdB`</summary>

```text
74	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
75	\label{sec:robustness_method}
76	
77	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position at $K{=}3$ are below the ${\geq}20$ threshold recommended by \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; at $K{=}5$, cluster count drops to ${\leq}11$, and at $K{=}7$ to ${\leq}9$. Following \citet{mackinnon2018wild}, we use Rademacher weights, which remain conservative in few-cluster settings even when cluster sizes are unequal. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count rather than absent effects. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$ structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}.
78	
```

</details>

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}}
4	\label{app:proof}
5	
6	The replaced judge $J_K'$ is \emph{pivotal} exactly when $t{-}1$ of the remaining $K{-}1$ judges have already failed. Formally, let $S = \sum_{j=1}^{K-1} X_j^a$ denote the number of failures among the shared judges. The panel fails if and only if the $K$-th judge also fails when $S = t{-}1$, or when $S \geq t$ regardless of the $K$-th judge:
7	\begin{align}
8	\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) &= \Pr(S \geq t) + \Pr(S = t{-}1) \cdot \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid S = t{-}1).
9	\end{align}
10	Since $\Pr(X_{K'}^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K}) \geq \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K})$ for all attacks $a$ and realizations $\mathbf{x}_{-K}$ (by hypothesis), the second term is weakly larger for $\mathcal{P}'$ than for $\mathcal{P}$ under every attack. Taking the supremum over attacks preserves the inequality: $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}') \geq \mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$. The result holds under arbitrary inter-judge correlations and any attack space $\mathcal{A}$.
11	
12	\section{Robustness Correction Methods}
13	\label{app:robustness_methods}
14	
15	The 455 panels are constructed combinatorially from 15 shared judges: each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ panels, inducing correlated residuals across panel-level observations. Standard OLS assumes independent errors, potentially inflating significance. We apply three corrections.
16	
17	\paragraph{Clustered Standard Errors.} Because each panel of $K{=}3$ judges does not map to a single cluster, we perform three separate one-way clusterings (one for each judge position in the ordered panel tuple) using CR1 bias-corrected sandwich estimators (13 clusters per position). The reported standard error for each coefficient is the maximum across the three clusterings, yielding the most conservative inference.
18	
19	\paragraph{Mixed Effects.} For each of the three judge positions, we fit a linear mixed-effects model with a random intercept for the judge occupying that position, estimating $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ as fixed effects. The reported $p$-value for each coefficient is the maximum across the three models.
20	
21	\paragraph{Wild Cluster Bootstrap.} We perform $B{=}9{,}999$ bootstrap resamples using Rademacher weights, clustering by each of the three panel positions in turn and taking the maximum $p$-value across positions (the most conservative aggregation). This approach avoids distributional assumptions on the cluster structure.
22	
23	\section{Extended Analysis}
24	\label{app:extended_analysis}
25	
26	\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}
27	\label{app:bft_reversal}
28	
29	Under standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\eta_{\max}$ significant in 10 of 12 conditions and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in all 12. Non-independence correction attenuates both predictors: under wild cluster bootstrap, $\eta_{\max}$ retains 3/12 and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 4/12, with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. The directional pattern (all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ point estimates positive) persists uniformly. The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship whose statistical confidence attenuates under adaptive attack (4/12 after correction).
30	
31	\begin{figure}[h]
32	\centering
33	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
34	\caption{GAM bootstrap $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction at $K{=}3$ ($B{=}9999$). All 12 conditions show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (88.9--100\% bootstrap rate). The Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$).}
35	\label{fig:gam_split}
36	\end{figure}
37	
38	Under corrected data (V2), all 12 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ OLS coefficients are positive. The previously reported negative ARC $\times$ sycophancy coefficient ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$) was an artifact of error-as-tie handling; after correction, it becomes positive ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = +0.257$). GAM bootstrap confirms the uniform positive direction: 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (88.9--100\% positive rate) and 12/12 at $K{=}5$ (100\%).
39	
40	\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}
41	\label{app:diversity_capability}
42	
43	The $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
44	
45	\begin{figure}[h]
46	\centering
47	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
48	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange diamonds, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
49	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
50	\end{figure}
51	
52	Family-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \citep{kuncheva2003measures}.
53	
54	\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}
55	\label{app:aggregation_details}
56	
57	Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and reduces overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS), providing partial defense. The mechanism is calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, reducing the diversity channel while the individual weakness channel remains active. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors highly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.829$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \citep{tambe2011security}.

63	\label{fig:aggregation}
64	\end{figure}

The Bayesian attenuation observed at $K{=}3$ (5+/1$-$; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}) does not persist: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reaches 7/12 regardless of aggregation rule. Predictability still varies ($\bar{R}^2$: threshold 0.408, Bayesian 0.166), but the diversity channel operates uniformly across all methods. At $K{=}7$, however, methods diverge again: Bayesian neutralization returns (0/12) while threshold voting strengthens to 10/12 (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k7}).

110	\subsection{Metric Robustness Under Partial Panel Information}
111	\label{app:oracle}
112	
113	The main analysis computes $\eta_{\max}$ with full knowledge of panel composition (all three judges known). In deployment, a practitioner selecting or auditing a panel may know only a subset of the constituent judges. We test whether $\eta_{\max}$ retains predictive power under such partial information by recomputing it from two of the three panel members: for each panel, we cycle through all $\binom{3}{1} = 3$ leave-one-out masks, compute $\eta_{\max}$ from the two known judges, and average the regression estimates across masking scenarios.
114	
115	Under the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\eta_{\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (4/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\eta_{\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\eta_{\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.

Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.50$) strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, comparable to the full-pool 7/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the shift weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 3--4 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).

159	\section{Random Subsample Analysis}
160	\label{app:random_subsample}
161	
162	To test whether the predictor shift depends on model pool composition, we randomly sample 9 of 15 models (1{,}000 trials), construct all $\binom{9}{5}$ panels, and run the bootstrap analysis at $K{=}5$. We stratify by the number of local models ($n_{\mathrm{local}} \in \{0, \ldots, 6\}$) in each subsample.
163	
164	Under clustered standard errors, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the dominant predictor in 51.4\% of subsamples (mean significance 1.85/12), with an inverted-U as a function of $n_{\mathrm{local}}$: subsamples with 3--4 local models (maximizing capability range) achieve the highest $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance rate, while all-API ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{=}0$) and heavily-local ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{\geq}5$) subsamples show reduced detection. Significance is attenuated relative to the full pool by smaller sample sizes (126 vs.\ 3{,}003 panels at $K{=}5$), but the shift direction holds in the majority of subsamples. This confirms that the shift's detectability depends on capability diversity in the model pool rather than pool size per se.

258	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
259	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
260	
261	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), both directions achieve 17/18 sign-consistent conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (5/6 at $K{=}3$, 6/6 at $K{\geq}5$). The sole exception in both directions is $K{=}3$ position bias, where all judges share similar ASR (${\sim}0.5$), leaving insufficient heterogeneity for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ to discriminate. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. While sign consistency is symmetric, cross-prediction $R^2$ is moderately asymmetric (ARC$\to$MMLU generally higher), suggesting that coefficients estimated from ARC's more constrained binary format generalize conservatively to MMLU's richer four-way choice distribution.
262	
263	\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}
264	\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}
265	
266	To test whether reducing the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.
267	
268	\begin{table}[h]
269	\centering
270	\small
271	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
272	\toprule
273	\textbf{Pool / $K$} & \textbf{RV $>$ $\eta$} & \textbf{Median RV} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\
274	\midrule
275	Full / $K{=}3$ & 1/12 & 0.30 & 1/12 \\
276	Full / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\
277	Pool A / $K{=}3$ & 2/12 & 0.25 & --- \\
278	Pool A / $K{=}5$ & 10/12 & 0.32 & 9/12 \\
279	\bottomrule
280	\end{tabular}
281	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\eta$: conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\ $0.81$ at $K{=}5$) increases the fraction of robust conditions from 1/12 to 2/12 at $K{=}3$ and from 3/12 to 10/12 at $K{=}5$, with the largest gains at $K{=}5$ where both benchmarks improve equally (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6).}
282	\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}
283	\end{table}
284	
285	At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 10/12 conditions (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6; the two failures are both sycophancy). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 10/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity substantially separates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.
286	
287	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
288	\label{app:epsilon}
289	
290	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions are preserved across $\epsilon$ values (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.073$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, all $p < 0.005$), confirming that the predictor shift finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 98.6\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (71/72 direction matches) (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
291	
292	\section{Temperature Sensitivity Pilot}
293	\label{app:temperature}
294	
295	We evaluate $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ stability across decoding temperatures $t \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.7, 1.0\}$ using a 3-model pilot (GPT-4.1-nano, Gemini-3.5-Flash, Claude-3-Haiku; 300 clean samples per benchmark, 3 repeats). The maximum $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation across all four temperatures is $\Delta < 0.034$ (MMLU: 0.033, ARC: 0.027), confirming that decoding randomness does not meaningfully perturb pairwise MI structure. Clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computation is thus robust to temperature variation within the range used by standard API configurations.
296	
297	\section{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Distribution Statistics}
298	\label{app:keff_dist}
299	
300	Table~\ref{tab:keff_dist} summarizes the distribution of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes.
301	
302	\begin{table}[h]
303	\centering
304	\small
305	\begin{tabular}{rrccc}
306	\toprule
307	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{Panels} & \textbf{Mean (SD)} & \textbf{Range} \\
308	\midrule
309	3 & 455     & 2.18 (0.30) & [1.47, 2.80] \\
310	5 & 3{,}003 & 3.35 (0.42) & [2.16, 4.30] \\
311	7 & 6{,}435 & 4.53 (0.47) & [2.97, 5.55] \\
312	\bottomrule
313	\end{tabular}
314	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ distribution across panel sizes. Mean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ increases approximately linearly with $K$ but remains below the theoretical maximum ($K_{\mathrm{eff}} = K$ for fully independent judges), reflecting non-zero inter-judge correlations.}
315	\label{tab:keff_dist}
316	\end{table}
317	
318	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits sufficient variance at each $K$ for regression analysis (coefficient of variation 10--14\%), with no floor or ceiling effects that would compress the predictor range.
319	
320	\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}
321	\label{app:power_analysis}
322	
323	Table~\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial significance at $K{=}3$ (4/12) is consistent with moderate power at this sample size; the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.
324	
325	\begin{table}[h]
326	\centering
327	\small
328	\begin{tabular}{rccc}
329	\toprule
330	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{Panels} & \textbf{MDE ($\beta_{\mathrm{std}}$)} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\
331	\midrule
332	3 & 455     & 0.144 & 4/12 \\
333	5 & 3{,}003 & 0.048 & 7/12 \\
334	7 & 6{,}435 & 0.036 & 8/12 \\
335	\bottomrule
336	\end{tabular}
337	\caption{Minimum detectable effect size (80\% power, 1{,}000 simulations, wild cluster bootstrap) by panel size. MDE decreases approximately as $1/\sqrt{N}$, confirming that $K{=}5$/$K{=}7$ significance reflects genuine effects rather than inflated type~I error.}
338	\label{tab:mde}
339	\end{table}
340	
341	\section{Alternative Diversity Measures}
342	\label{app:alt_diversity}
343	
344	We compare $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ against three classical ensemble diversity measures using the same OLS specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) with FDR correction across 12 conditions pooled over $K$ values.
345	
346	\begin{table}[h]
347	\centering
348	\small
349	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
350	\toprule
351	\textbf{Measure} & \textbf{Sig} & \textbf{Dir.} & \textbf{$|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|$} & $\boldsymbol{\rho}$ \textbf{w/} $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\
352	\midrule
353	$Q$-statistic    & 9/12  & 6+/3$-$ & 0.251 & 0.599 \\
354	Disagreement     & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$ & 0.481 & 0.934 \\
355	$\kappa$-diversity & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$ & 0.497 & 0.978 \\
356	\addlinespace
357	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (ref.) & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$ & 0.430 & --- \\
358	\bottomrule
359	\end{tabular}
360	\caption{Comparison of diversity measures as predictors of panel ASR (OLS, FDR-corrected, 12 conditions pooled across $K$). Sig: FDR-significant conditions. $|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|$: mean absolute standardized coefficient. $\rho$: Pearson correlation with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$.}
361	\label{tab:alt_diversity}
362	\end{table}
363	
364	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} are near-equivalent ($\rho{=}0.978$), with comparable significance counts (12/12 vs.\ 11/12) and effect sizes (0.430 vs.\ 0.497). Disagreement rate achieves similar predictive power ($\rho{=}0.934$, 11/12). The $Q$-statistic is the weakest predictor (9/12, $|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.251$), consistent with its known insensitivity to the type of error correlation that is associated with panel vulnerability \citep{kuncheva2003measures}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s advantage over these alternatives is not predictive power but interpretability: it quantifies the number of effective independent channels in the panel, connecting directly to the information-theoretic foundation of panel aggregation (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
365	
366	\section{$\kappa$-Diversity Predictor Shift Comparison}
367	\label{app:kappa_regime}
368	
369	To test whether the predictor shift (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}) is specific to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ or a generic property of diversity measures, we repeat the wild cluster bootstrap analysis replacing $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ with $\kappa$-diversity ($1 - \bar{\kappa}$, where $\bar{\kappa}$ is the mean pairwise Cohen's $\kappa$ on clean winner labels; $\rho{=}0.978$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$).
370	
371	\begin{table}[h]
372	\centering
373	\small
374	\begin{tabular}{rcccc}
375	\toprule
376	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{$\kappa$-diversity}} \\
377	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
378	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{div.\ sig} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & \textbf{div.\ sig} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} \\
379	\midrule
380	3 & 4/12  & 3/12 & 8/12 & 1/12 \\
381	5 & 7/12 & 2/12 & 7/12 & 4/12 \\
382	7 & 8/12  & 1/12 & 6/12 & 1/12 \\
383	\bottomrule
384	\end{tabular}
385	\caption{Predictor shift comparison: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ $\kappa$-diversity across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a clear shift ($4 \to 7 \to 8$); $\kappa$-diversity significance decreases monotonically ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), showing no predictor shift.}
386	\label{tab:kappa_regime}
387	\end{table}
388	
389	$\kappa$-diversity is a stronger predictor than $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\ 4/12), consistent with its larger mean effect size ($|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.497$ vs.\ 0.430; Table~\ref{tab:alt_diversity}). However, $\kappa$-diversity shows no predictor shift: its significance \emph{decreases} from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}7$ ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), the opposite of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s pattern ($4 \to 7 \to 8$). Furthermore, under $\kappa$-diversity, $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance at $K{=}5$ (4/12), whereas under $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ it drops to 2/12. The predictor shift---co-determination at $K{=}3$ transitioning to diversity dominance at $K{\geq}5$---is thus specific to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s MI-based formulation, which captures effective judge independence rather than pairwise label agreement.
390	
391	\section{Gaussian Copula Assumption and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Robustness}
392	\label{app:copula_gof}
393	
394	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ admits a closed-form expression $K - \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i}\sum_{j \neq i} \rho_{ij}^2$ under the Gaussian copula, where $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \rho_{ij}^2$ \citep{nelsen2006copulas}. We test whether this parametric assumption holds for judge pairs using the \citet{genest2008validity} Cram\'{e}r--von Mises goodness-of-fit test for Gaussian copulas.
395	
396	\paragraph{GoF Results.} Across all $\binom{15}{2} = 105$ judge pairs, the Gaussian copula is rejected at $\alpha{=}0.05$ for 97--100\% of pairs (MMLU: 102/105, 97.1\%; ARC: 104/105, 99.0\%). The rejections are not marginal: median $p$-values are ${<}0.001$ on both benchmarks. This indicates that the pairwise dependence structure among LLM judges is substantially non-Gaussian, likely reflecting the discrete three-category nature of winner labels and the heavy tail structure of judge agreement patterns.
397	
398	\paragraph{Ranking Robustness Despite Copula Rejection.} Despite the Gaussian copula being formally rejected, the closed-form $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $1 - \rho_{ij}^2$) and the plug-in MI $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $\exp(-2\,\widehat{\mathrm{MI}}(i,j))$ with Miller--Madow correction) produce highly concordant panel rankings within each $K$. Within-$K$ Spearman rank correlations are $\rho \approx 0.86$ ($K{=}3$: 0.862, $K{=}5$: 0.858, $K{=}7$: 0.867). The systematic level difference (plug-in $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ mean 3.38 vs.\ closed-form 4.29, averaged across $K$) reflects the Miller--Madow bias correction increasing MI estimates, which compresses $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ toward zero. This level shift does not affect regression results, which depend on ranking.
399	
400	\paragraph{Implications.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effectiveness as a predictor of panel vulnerability does not depend on the Gaussian copula being correct. The $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ kernel provides a nonparametric, information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation; the Gaussian copula merely offers a convenient closed-form approximation that preserves ranking fidelity. All regression analyses in the main text use the closed-form $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$; the plug-in MI version would yield equivalent conclusions for any analysis based on panel ordering.
401
